It is a form of social engineering , there 's no variety . But the unit blocks we 're getting now are not variety ; it 's one and two-bedroom units . There are no three-bedroom units for families . They do n't exist . <p> It 's a form of social engineering . It 's pushing families to the outskirts of the city . The newer flats tend to be smaller than the older-style flats ; the older-style unit blocks from the seventies tend to have a little bit of garden , they 're not quite as concrete . Basically , developers are cutting costs as much as they can . <p> And in terms of environmental and social arguments we have to be careful . At the level of five stories you can still have a relationship with the street . You can look out the window and see the kids playing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ with the street . From the point of view of community , that is an important thing . <p> Second , once you go above four or five stories , you have to install a lift and the building starts to be environmentally unsustainable . Now that a lot of old factories are going and really big parcels of land are starting to be developed into really big unit blocks , you are now starting to see not only flats just on the edge of buildings where there is a window onto the outside world but flats in the middle of these big buildings where there is no window to the outside world , where there is no natural light , where there is no natural ventilation . And that is very problematic . <p> My observation of residents ' groups -- while it 's true you can have residents ' groups that are very nimbyish and self-centred , and you can have the populationists enter into them and try to turn these groups in a right-wing direction -- most of the residents I 've come across who are protesting over-development @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ want development that is n't just jammed in . They want to see the right sorts of development , in the right sorts of places . <p> We need to take development , most sorts of development , out of the hands of the private developers so that you can genuinely have variety . <p> There is also a view that if you allow lots of high rise it will automatically create affordable housing . This is n't true . If it was , the cost of housing would be decreasing . Housing affordability is affected by speculation in the market and high rise leads to more speculation , not less . Most of these units and flats are completely unaffordable for ordinary people . <p> You only need to look at Docklands to see that . Many of the units in Docklands are unoccupied . Either people ca n't afford to live in them or else people who can afford to live in them do n't want to because it 's a very un-community kind of area . It 's a place without a soul . Usually the only surface that is n't concrete is some pebbles and a few spikey plants out the front . Basically they are making the suburbs hotter . This also creates a flooding issue . There 's not enough soil to absorb heavy rainfall so you get flash floods . <p> There need to be laws that force developers to plant trees , for instance , trees with a canopy . There are a range of things that really need to be done to try and prevent these developments being so unsustainable environmentally . <p> One of the big constraints on councils involves finances . Rates are n't means tested . A worker or a welfare recipient may have inherited a house , be living in it and then have to pay the same rates as a wealthy neighbour . It 's a very inequitable form of taxation . <p> Generally , I have voted against raising rates above the level of inflation . That did n't get up mainly because the state government subsidises the concession to pensioners but is n't willing to do the same for other welfare recipients . What sort of support or collaboration is there ? <p> I have preferred to remain quite separate and independent . On certain issues , especially around climate change , I can work with the Greens . On issues such as outsourcing , privatisation and socially progressive issues I can work with one of the more moderate ALP councillors . <p> What do you see as the big issues facing the council in 2015 and beyond ? <p> One big issue is climate change and dealing with heat-affected people and working out how to help relieve the stress . I think that is a huge issue going into the future . <p> I also think the lack of affordable housing for people in the area is a big issue . The council needs to do what it can to try and push for genuinely affordable public housing . <p> Another big issue is poverty in the community as a result of the neoliberal cuts by the various governments . We 've got loss of jobs , unemployment , especially in the north of Moreland . We 've @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ seriously increased in over 20 or 30 years . We 've also got people on bridging visas living on 89% of Newstart . We 've got international students and people with particular migration statuses where they are not entitled to any kind of government help at all and who ca n't find work . Then there are people on the minimum wage or even below the minimum wage . <p> There is a perception of Moreland being a white-collar professional area but actually there are two Morelands . There is the white-collar professional Moreland but there is also a working-class or blue-collar Moreland , which is often overlooked by council . So a lot of solutions council looks to in terms of jobs are much more oriented to the white-collar professional sectors rather than the working-class ones . <p> It 's also important to retain industrial land within the Moreland area so that there is a possibility of getting blue-collar jobs close to home . There would be a lot of workers in Moreland who would work at Ford or in the auto components industry that 's dependent on Ford . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ workers wo n't be going into some office , they 're not going to become lawyers , they need similar sorts of work to what they had . <p> I also smell a push in Moreland for more of a user-pays commercialisation approach , especially with the hire of meeting rooms to groups . The argument will be that Moreland has to get money somehow , therefore it needs to charge more for council services . <p> A question on the workload of a councillors . I am always struck by the massive stack of agenda papers for council meetings . Councillors are paid a very small amount and there is only one monthly meeting . Do you think these arrangements are sustainable ? <p> Actually the amount that councillors are paid at Moreland is much more substantial that at some other councils . It 's currently at $26,000 per year . There are some minimum-wage jobs that pay less than that . So it is feasible to combine a councillor position with a part-time job . Most councillors are full-time workers or have flexible jobs or maybe work three or @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on previous councils were staffers ( for state and federal MPs ) before the Local Government Act banned this after a scandal in the Brimbank council in 2009 . <p> It puts pressure on councillors to just vote through reports . Unless you 've got personal experience of something or a resident has contacted you , you might not realise that something is a problem . <p> It would be better if councillors were actually full time and doing the work itself so you do n't have a separation between councillors and the council administration . <p> One thing makes me feel very positive about the prospects for socialism and humanity . There are lots of issues that come at you all the time when you are elected to one of these positions . You wo n't have all of the answers . Residents raise all sorts of issues and a lot of residents have a lot of very good ideas and they put in a lot of research and thought and effort into all sorts of issues . <p> What this means is that I do n't have to come @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ have to have a policy on absolutely everything . <p> As long as you are guided by the principle of people before profit , anti-privatisation , opposition to outsourcing , you do n't have to have an answer to everything because often residents will find things out , they 'll do the research . Obviously there is politics involved but it gives you faith that people are capable of running society themselves . <p> There is some discussion among Victorian councils about more participatory budgeting . There is an experiment at Melbourne City Council and at Darebin . I would like to find out more about the how the socialist movement in Porto Alegre in Brazil did it . <p> My impression is that the way that participatory budgeting is being put forward in Australia is very tokenistic , especially the one in Melbourne which involved 100 residents being chosen ( presumably by the council ) to particpate in discussing the budget . <p> I do n't know exactly how they did it in Darebin but they allocated an amount of money that a pool of residents could decide how it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ kind of thing is done in the Philippines in some of the left-wing barangays , which is equivalent to our local council , where there is a general assembly -- and they have to campaign to get everyone to attend and you might have 1000 or 1500 people -- and they vote on what to do . <p> That 's much better than what happens here where the gallery of residents observes what the council is doing . It 's actually a very disconcerting thing where you address your arguments to other councillors in terms of how they should vote but the residents are just observers . <p> When you do letterboxing in Moreland for various elections you realise how vast and disparate Moreland actually is . There are some 150,000 residents and a many different areas . Moreland was created by Kennett 's shotgun amalgamations in the early 1990s . Do you think such a large council area is compatible with more grassroots democracy ? <p> I was talking with some residents who were saying that the current South Ward , which covers Brunswick south of Albion Street , used @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ much stronger engagement between residents and their local councillor when there were three single-member wards . Now that whole area is a multi-member ward with three councillors . <p> Different councils in Melbourne have different structures . Some have single-member wards and some have multi-member wards . Multi-member wards with proportional representation are certainly more advantageous to the left than single-member wards . <p> Sam Wainwright in Fremantle won in a single-member ward but in a more working-class area , and they were able to do a deal with the Greens that the Greens would n't run against him and we would n't run against them . We would n't be able to do anything similar in Moreland . <p> I suspect that if we had single-member wards it would be hard to get elected against the Labor Party unless you had a huge base of support . <h> Comments <p> * The campaign against the East-West Link succeeded because of the good match between the different campaign groups and the different tasks that the movement faced . The movement needed both to get rid of the Liberals and to get @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The Public Transport Users Association was focused on marginal seat campaigning and would have had an effect in terms of convincing the public at large that improving public transport was a better use of public money than building the road . This helped Labor and was one of the things ( there were many others , but I suspect that public transport was the biggest single issue ) that helped it get elected . <p> While the PTUA 's Public Transport Not Traffic campaign was necessary work , it was n't sufficient . The campaign that Sue discussed ( MCAT , YCAT &amp; the Tunnel Pickets ) exploited the competition in inner Melbourne between Labor and the Greens . Labor 's position was that it did n't want the East-West Link , but its hands would be tied if the contracts were signed before the election . Campaigners were especially suspicious of this position because of the power of well-known supporters of the roads lobby like Tim Pallas , who was Shadow Treasurer . Labor was eventually forced , not long before election day , to come out and say that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ change of mind could only have come about with internal polling that showed they were going to lose the whole of inner Melbourne to the Greens if they did n't . The Councils ' court case was also useful , because it gave Labor a pretext to use and say it was n't actually going to break a contract . The campaign of MCAT , YCAT &amp; the Tunnel Pickets was the essential element that created the situation where the East-West Link was electoral poison in the inner suburbs of Melbourne , thus putting the ALP in the position where it had to shift its position . <p> * Sue 's position of supporting increased density , but opposing it being driven by developers is correct . It is absolutely unsustainable for Melbourne to keep expanding outwards - it is one of the least dense cities of a comparable size in the world . Not only does this lead to car dependency , but it also produces social isolation and is incredibly expensive to service with public infrastructure . Letting developers have open slather to build high rises , however , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and also produces social isolation . What is needed is well designed medium density housing . Private developers , however , are n't interested in that - and , if they do build a well designed medium density development , they 'd sell it at such a high price that working class people could n't afford it . There is , therefore , no solution within capitalism for this problem - like so many others . <p> * Council rates are basically a tax on one form of wealth - real estate . Under capitalism , there will always be people who are asset rich but cash poor , so while there should be mechanisms to cope with this , a tax on wealth is n't something that the Left should oppose . It would be better , though , if rates were levied on the unimproved land value , rather than land + building , because that would punish landbanking and assist in urban consolidation . Finally , how progressive or regressive any individual tax is should be regarded as distinctly secondary to how progressive or regressive the overall tax @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ regressive effect on the tax system in Australia is the GST , because of its intensely regressive design and the fact that it takes in such a massive amount of money . I think an examination of council rates across Australia would discover that they are mildly progressive overall , though with a highly variable impact . <p> * In the early 90s , before the council amalgamations , the Left had cleaned out the Right from the Brunswick ALP . Their performance was such a massive improvement over the previous lot that they proceeded to win every one of the 12 single-member wards on Brunswick Council . As a result , they abolished caucus . They were embarrassed about the result , though , and requested the State government to bring in multi-member wards . <p> Thanks for the thoughtful comments . There is just one comment that I wanted to comment on which is the issue of Council rates and taxes . Capitalist Australia is moving towards more and more regressive taxes ( GST &amp; plans to increase the GST ) , cutting corporate taxes to next to nothing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ) and stripping the progressive income tax system of its progressive element by not indexing the tax brackets when workers win wage rises . <p> Each level of government is cost shifting onto other levels of government . For example , Moreland 's libraries used to receive around 70% of funding from the state government and now they only receive around 30% funding from the state government . Now the Victorian government has introduced rate capping at the CPI . This will result in the axing of local council services , the loss of jobs and dilapidated infrastructure . However , council rates are very regressive , especially when we live under a capitalist system which tells people that you can only have housing security if you take out a gigantic mortgage . The council rates do n't distinguish between rich and poor . <p> In reality , the local governments should wage a campaign against the state and federal governments to release adequate funding to cover local government services and infrastructure . 
@@3214375 <h> Authors <p> Research Manager , The Climate Council of Australia and Honorary Associate , Department of Environment and Geography , Macquarie University <h> Disclosure statement <p> Will Steffen is a Climate Councillor . The Climate Council is an independent non-profit organisation funded by donations from the public . Its mission is to provide authoritative , expert advice to the Australian public on climate change . <p> Martin Rice is the Research Manager of the Climate Council , an independent non-profit organisation funded by donations from the public . Its mission is to provide authoritative , expert advice to the Australian public on climate change . <p> The new report uses a " carbon budget " approach . This figures out how much carbon can be let into the atmosphere while limiting warming to 2C , and is a different method to the " targets and timetables " method favoured by international climate negotiations . These are are highly complex and difficult to interpret and put into practice . <p> A carbon budget instead shows how much carbon humanity can spend without blowing the budget . And the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ more serious climate impacts become . <p> Climate Council <h> Time is running out <p> To track emissions against the warming limit of 2C above the pre-industrial level , the most commonly used method in the policy world is the " targets and timetables " approach . This is often based on a target reduction in greenhouse gas emissions by a certain date or over a specified period . <p> Examples of targets and timetables include the United States target of reducing emissions by 26-28% by 2025 against a 2005 baseline and Australia 's target of reducing emissions unconditionally by 5% by 2020 against a 2000 baseline , and conditionally by 15-25% depending on international action . <p> In practice , the complexity of the targets and timetables approach , especially the number of variations , makes it difficult to compare the level of effort of one country against another and to assess combined international efforts to stabilise the climate system . <h> Flipping the carbon coin <p> The carbon budget is a scientifically robust , conceptually simple way of estimating how much more CO2 we can emit to the atmosphere @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a single , globally aggregated amount of CO2 that can be emitted before the world 's economy must be decarbonised . Other greenhouse gases such as methane and nitrous oxide are accounted for in the construction of the budget . <p> The higher the probability we want of preventing a global temperature rise of 2C , the more stringent the budget . For instance , if we want a 50% chance ( the toss of a coin ) of limiting warming to 2C , the budget from 2012 is 1112 billion tonnes of CO2 . <p> For a 66% chance , the budget is 1,010 billion tonnes of CO2 . And for a 75% chance , the budget is 672 billion tonnes of CO2 . If we want a greater than 75% probability of not exceeding the 2C warming limit , the carbon budget drops sharply to values much lower than 672 billion tonnes of CO2 . <p> To keep this challenge in perspective , current human emissions of CO2 are about 36 billion tonnes per year . At this rate , we would burn through the budget by 2032 if @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <h> What does this mean for fossil fuels ? <p> From setting a carbon budget we can work backwards to figure out how much coal , oil and gas can be extracted and burned . <p> An initial study in 2013 by the Carbon Tracker and Grantham Institute 2013 compared the known global fossil fuel reserves ( coal , oil and gas ) with the carbon budget . They estimated that if all of the world 's indicated reserves of fossil fuels were burned , 2,860 billion tonnes of CO2 would be emitted to the atmosphere . This is more than 2.5 times greater than the allowed budget for a toss-of-the-coin chance of succeeding . <p> Recently , Christophe McGlade and Paul Ekins at University College London examined further the implications of the carbon budget for the use of fossil fuels . They used an economic optimisation method : how to gain the most economic value from the limited amount of coal , oil and gas that can be burned , and in what regions . <p> This analysis , again based on just a 50:50 chance of preventing a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ global coal reserves , 52% of gas reserves and 35% of oil reserves are unburnable and must be left in the ground . <p> Put simply , tackling climate change requires that most of the world 's fossil fuels be left in the ground , unburned . <h> Australia 's beached coal whales <p> According to the study mentioned above , for Australia to play its role in preventing a 2C rise in temperature requires more than 90% of our coal reserves to be left in the ground unburned . <p> Energy policies that continue to support substantial fossil fuel use are inconsistent with tackling climate change . A case in point is the proposed coal expansion in the Galilee Basin , which is not viable economically if the carbon budget is respected . The Galilee coal is lower quality and high cost to extract , meaning under a carbon budget limiting warming to 2C other reserves would be more viable , leaving other assets stranded like beached whales . <p> For example , many of Australia 's coal-fired power plants are inefficient and nearing the end of their lifetimes , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ rooftop solar and wind continue to fall . Work by ClimateWorks Australia and ANU shows Australia can decarbonise the economy with little or no cost through energy efficiency , low-carbon electricity ( renewables , nuclear and carbon capture and storage ) , and electrification and fuel switching ( from petrol to electricity or biofuel ) . <p> With the carbon budget rapidly running out , it is urgent that global emissions begin to track downward in the next few years . Ultimately , most of the world 's fossil fuels must be left in the ground , unburned.

@@3214448 <h> Lisa Cox , Tom Arup <p> Australia has been urged to rapidly accelerate its cuts to greenhouse gases , with the independent Climate Change Authority recommending the Abbott government adopt an ambitious 30 per cent reduction target on 2000 levels by 2025 . <p> In a report to be published on Wednesday , the authority has also declared Australia is falling far short of the task required to cut emissions by 2020 if it wants to match the efforts of other countries to halt global warming . <p> It comes as the world 's biggest emitters , including the US and China , were revealed this week to have questioned the credibility of Australia 's climate change targets and the Abbott government 's direct action policy to pay polluters to reduce emissions . <p> Advertisement <p> The Climate Change Authority -- an independent body set up by Labor to advise on climate policy -- says Australia has no grounds for special pleading for lower targets than other countries as part of a new global climate agreement . <p> It is also critical of the government for " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ generations in its recent Intergenerational Report , which sets out challenges governments will face in their budgets over the next 40 years . <p> The authority released its report two months before a government taskforce , set up in the Department of Prime Minister and Cabinet , is expected to hand down its recommendations on what Australia 's post-2020 emissions reduction targets should be . <p> The government will announce Australia 's targets mid-year , six months out from a global climate summit in Paris where it is hoped a new climate agreement will be reached . <p> Climate Change Authority chair Bernie Fraser said on Tuesday that the authority 's recommendation for a target of 30 per cent on 2000 levels by 2025 was " broadly comparable with the targets that other wealthy developed countries will be bringing to the table in Paris " to limit global warming to 2 degrees . <p> " If Australia were to pledge the recommended 30 per cent target at the Paris conference this would likely be considered the behaviour of a good global citizen , and go some way to answering those who @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ recent times , " Mr Fraser said . <p> The authority 's report says a target year of 2025 would give Australia flexibility if it had to ramp up its efforts to cut emissions in the years to 2030 . <p> It says Australia should set a target to cut emissions by 40 to 60 per cent on 2000 levels by 2030 . <p> It recommends beefing up Australia 's 2020 target to 19 per cent , which would better match global efforts and mean governments would not have to scale up their policies as drastically to cut emissions in the years after . <p> The report challenges arguments made by the government that because Australia 's emissions constitute a small proportion of total global emissions , actions taken by Australia will not make a great difference to the overall global picture . <p> It states Australia is still the 13th largest emitter in the world at 1.3 per cent of total global emissions . <p> But with just 0.4 per cent of the world 's population Australia is the largest emitter on a per capita basis among developed countries , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ three times the per capita emissions of Germany . <p> Mr Fraser said : " The assertion that , as a small emitter , Australia could sit on the sidelines of this particular global contest was always more self-serving than credible . <p> " To maintain that posture in the light of increasing international actions to reduce emissions -- by developed and developing , big and small countries -- makes it even less credible . " <p> As the driest continent Australia is particularly vulnerable to the effects of climate change , making it even more important for the government to match the ambition of other countries , the authority said . <p> The report was given to media outlets ahead of its release on conditions that prevented Fairfax Media from seeking comment from the Abbott government . <h> 191 comments <p> Australia is currently cutting carbon emissions . @@3214502 <p> The winners of the fourth Global Holcim Awards for Sustainable Construction have been announced , and include a University of Melbourne graduate Milinda Pathiraja , who took home the second prize alongside her Robust Architecture Workshop colleague , Ganga Ratnayake . <p> Selected from 15 global finalists , which in turn were whittled down from an initial 6,000 entries , three projects in Colombia , Sri Lanka and the United States were awarded a total of US$350,000 in prize money . <p> All three recognise architectural interventions that deliver tangible benefits to local communities , which the forward-looking program seeks to promote in each three-year cycle . <p> Taking home the GOLD was ' Articulated Site ' by Mario Camargo and Luis Tomb ? of Colectivo720 in Cali , together with Juan Calle and Horacio Valencia of EPM Group ( Empresas P ? blicas de Medell ? n ) . <p> Centred on creating spaces around and above a series of water reservoirs , the project for a public park in Medell ? n , Colombia transforms existing public space and elements to create an outdoor auditorium @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ important resource of urban life . <p> The project was praised for forming a " socio-technical " landscape , its architectural form taking inspiration from the site 's history , surrounding topography , and structure of the existing tanks and pools . Special attention was also given to water management , utilising recycling technologies involving rainwater and grey water harvesting for irrigation of the park . <p> " The jury applauds the careful integration of the ensemble into the physical and social fabric of Medell ? n -- in a scheme that is a model for best practice that could be emulated by other cities in Latin America and around the globe " , said head of the awards jury and Dean of the Graduate School of Design at Harvard University , Mohsen Mostafavi . <p> Reuse of the existing water reservoir as public space for encounters <p> The SILVER prize went to ' Post-War Collective ' by Milinda Pathiraja and Ganga Ratnayake of Robust Architecture Workshop . Located in the rural town of Ambepussa near Colombo , Sri Lanka , the project aims to reintegrate soldiers into post-war Sri Lankan @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ construction of a community library . <p> The library , sitting lightly on the landscape , wraps around an inner courtyard and is made of rammed earth walls and recycled materials . <p> " There is significant value in the basic message of the scheme -- and the construction of a library that builds both the physical and social fabric of a community , " commented Mostafavi . <p> Specific steel welding joints have been strategically incorporated in the building . These joints provide the technical platforms to incrementally transfer welding and cutting skills to the workforce . <p> A consortium headed by BIG -- Bjarke Ingels Group and One Architecture won the BRONZE award for a large-scale integrated floor protection system designed to address the vulnerability of New York City to coastal flooding . Named ' Dryline ' , the project proposes a protective ribbon in Southern Manhattan using a series of raised berms and other measures to create public spaces along the water 's edge . <p> Although the judges debated whether the project was more reactive rather than proactive , addressing the effects of climate change rather than @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ turning a problem into an opportunity . <p> " The project makes a political statement by means of an architectural and urban proposition -- where tangible solutions to the effects of climate change can be created , where New York City is a prototype from which similar strategies in susceptible regions around the globe could be pursued " , he said . <p> The ' BIG U ' contains a protective ribbon : 21 km ( 13 mi ) of flood protection tailored to each neighborhood and the community it serves .

@@3217611 <p> Climate change contrarian Bjorn Lomborg , who is launching an Australian arm of his Copenhagen Consensus Center think tank after a $4million grant from the Australian Government Photograph : Martin Godwin <p> Danish political scientist and climate change contrarian Bj ? rn Lomborg says the poorest countries in the world need coal and climate change just is n't as big a problem as some people make out . <p> Australia 's Prime Minister Tony Abbott says " coal is good for humanity " and there are more pressing problems in the world than climate change , which he once described as " crap " but now says he accepts . <p> So it 's not surprising then that the latter should furnish the former with $4 million of taxpayer funds to start an Australian arm of Lomborg 's Copenhagen Consensus Centre ( CCC ) at the University of Western Australia 's business school . <p> The CCC has consistently said that targets to cut greenhouse gas emissions are too expensive and money should be spent elsewhere <p> After a couple of weeks of doubt and confusion over @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ reports suggest that the idea came from the Prime Minister 's office . <p> A spokesperson for the Prime Minister told Fairfax media it was " the government 's decision to bring the Lomborg consensus methodology to Australia " . <p> More on this " methodology " and some pretty fundamental problems with it in a bit . <p> Former government climate commissioner Tim Flannery , whose Climate Commission was defunded when the Abbott government won office , said the funding was " an insult to the scientific community " . <h> A $4m methodology ? <p> So is this " methodology " the Abbott Government has spent $4million on any good ? <p> Broadly , Lomborg claims that by doing a cost-benefit analysis of various policies to attack global issues , you can work out where you get the most " good " for the money you spend . <p> " What we are doing is economics , not science , " Lomborg told ABC Radio earlier this week , with no hint of irony . <p> Dr Frank Jotzo , director of the Centre for Climate Ecnomics and Policy @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ write a paper for Lomborg 's centre in 2008 , which was sharply critical of how the cost of the impacts of climate change were treated . <p> He told me : <p> Within the research community , particularly within the economics community , the Bjorn Lomborg enterprise has no academic credibility . It is seen as an outreach activity that is driven by specific set of objectives in terms of bringing particular messages into the public debate and in some cases making relatively extreme positions seem more acceptable in the public debate . <p> The Copenhagen Consensus Center 's Vice President Roland Mathiasson responded , saying they worked " with more than 100 of the world 's top economists , seven Nobel Laureates and Cambridge University Press " and " I do n't think they agree with Dr. Frank Jotzo 's statement that their work has " no academic credibility .. <p> Jotzo added : <p> My reference to lack of academic credibility relates to how the ' Consensus ' exercises are set up , not to the work of those invited to contribute to them . The exercises are @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , when in reality the sensible course of action will almost always be a mix of different objectives or courses of action . The problem with this framing of false alternatives was called out , for example , by a group of academics who contributed to the 2008 Consensus exercise in a paper published in the journal Climate Change Economics . The same occurs in the " Post-2015 " exercise , for example on climate change where some options get summarily dismissed on the basis of scant analysis , when in reality a portfolio approach would clearly be superior . This is not the making of the expert panel , it is inherent in the way in which the exercise is set up . <p> Lomborg has said that his Australia Consensus Centre will look at climate change , but it wo n't be a big part of what it does . <p> One reason for that is because Lomborg 's work over the years has routinely underplayed the importance of climate change and its impacts . <p> Jotzo told me there are two key ways in which Lomborg 's methods @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> Firstly , it uses low estimates of the cost of each tonne of carbon dioxide using economic modeling techniques which fail to properly price the impacts of climate change . <p> Estimates of this " social cost of carbon " vary massively . A study led by Stanford University scientists in January , for example , tried to better represent the costs of climate change impacts and found that each tonne of carbon dioxide had a cost to society of $220 , compared to US government estimates of about $37 . <p> Secondly , these economic methods allow for the use of what 's known as " discount rates " . <p> For example , if you buy a car that uses less fuel but costs more to buy , people will tend to discount the benefit because it takes a few years to get back the extra money you spent . <p> In short , it 's a way of putting a price on human impatience ( imagine telling a person living in 50 years time that you decided not to cut emissions in 2015 because you thought @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> On the point of discount rates , Jotzo is blunt : <p> There is no broadly ethical case that can be made for present generations to be so selfish to say that we will not invest in mitigating climate change because the benefits of our investment today will accrue to future generations and not to us . <p> What 's more , Jotzo says that while cost-benefit analysis can be useful , it does n't work when you apply it to climate change policy . <p> How do you price , for example , the loss of a Pacific island nation and what that would mean for the cultures that have thrived there ? What 's the price losing multiple species of flora and fauna or the Great Barrier Reef Jotzo adds : <p> Climate change is exceptional because it has all of these dimensions that go beyond the practical capability of cost benefit analysis . <h> Lomborg 's funding <p> Exactly how and where Bjorn Lomborg 's think tank has gathered its cash over the years has been a tough story to get the bottom of . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ out in 2012 , Lomborg had already registered the US arm of the think tank four years earlier . <p> Since 2008 , the US tax records of the Copenhagen Consensus Center show it has gathered about $5million in income , more than half of which had come in 2012 and 2013 ( the most recent years for which records are available ) . <p> Lomborg himself was paid $975,000 via the think tank in those two years . <p> Yet much of the think tank 's income is not disclosed because , according to the think tank , " given how some parts of the blogosphere vilify Dr Lomborg " this is the sort of attention those donors can do without . <p> My own research on DeSmog has found that many of the earlier donors to Lomborg 's US think tank have been linked to the conservative side of politics , some with links to the billionaire Koch brothers . <p> I did n't choose to make a big deal of those links to the oil billionaire Koch brothers , but others , such as prominent US climate commentator @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ will be looking to raise further cash . In my view , this is for good reason . <p> For example , Lomborg has claimed that a recent slowdown in the rise of globally average temperatures is a sure sign that climate change computer models that predict higher temperature rises in the future are probably wrong . <p> Yet there is a plethora of studies in the literature explaining the " fake pause " and they mostly relate to the way some climate @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ world 's climate , such as volcanic eruptions and La Nina climate cycles , which can both have a cooling influence on glob al temperatures . <p> Lomborg likes to pick points in time when climate indicators superficially seem to support his arguments that climate change might not be quite so bad . <p> In 2008 , for example , Lomborg wrote an opinion article in The Guardian . I pick this older article because sufficient time has now passed to give us a crude test of Lomborg 's predictive ability . Was he right to highlight the things he chose to highlight ? <p> Lomborg wrote : " Since 1992 , we have had satellites measuring the rise in global sea levels , and they have shown a stable increase of 3.2mm per year ( 1/8 of an inch ) -- spot on compared to the IPCC projection . Moreover , over the last two years , sea levels have not increased at all -- actually , they show a slight drop . Should we not be told that this is much better than expected ? " <p> What Lomborg @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ level rise for the 20th century was 1.7mm per year and that it was now rising faster ( he could have got this from the IPCC report which came out the previous year ) . <p> Talking of ocean heat , in the same column Lomborg said this measure was " arguably " more important than others , such as the globally averaged temperature . <p> He told readers that " the heat content of the world 's oceans has been dropping for the past four years " and that " this is again much better than expected " . <p> In actual fact , if you look again at the IPCC 's assessment the previous year , the ocean heat content of the oceans had not been " dropping " but had been rising a little slower than previously . <p> But what has happened to ocean heat content since then ? @@3217644 <p> Oil companies like Shell have unique skills that make them ideally placed to help build the energy world of the future . Yet they seem unable to look beyond their own interests , says Adriaan Kamp , former Shell manager and founder of Oslo-based consultancy Energy For One World . " Like the banks , they ca n't change their ways . They are still making too much money with oil and gas . " According to Kamp , the growth strategy of the oil companies is still based exclusively on expanding their oil and gas business -- in Shell 's case most of all LNG . " They leave it to others to come up with the real solutions . " Kamp is convinced this strategy is doomed to failure . " The present energy system can not continue as it is . " <p> To explain where the oil and gas industry stands in the energy transition debate , Adriaan Kamp tells a little anecdote . " Every year in Oslo there is the Oslo Energy Forum . This is a kind of mini-Davos for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ year the Forum 's Managing Director , Johan Nic Vold , ex-Shell executive , held a speech , which he showed to me . It essentially said : we need the Elon Musks of this world , the Apples and the Googles , to come up with radical solutions . We , oil and gas companies , ca n't do it . " <p> I had asked Kamp for an interview because I wanted to find out from him how oil companies like Shell are viewing their future , knowing that much of their fossil fuel reserves will have to stay in the ground -- or may lead to runaway global warming . <p> " Oil people still live in a bubble . They lead a very good life " <p> If anyone should be in a position to address this question , it 's Kamp . Trained as a physicist at the Technical University Delft ( the same Alma Mater as that of Shell 's current CEO , Ben van Beurden , who is a chemical engineer ) , Kamp joined Shell in 1987 . His first job was to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Later he supervised upstream projects and developed new businesses ( including unconventional plays ) in Norway , Syria and , Scotland . After some years outside of the company , he returned to Shell to work at the head office in The Hague , where he cooperated with Shell 's famous " scenarios " team . However , increasingly uncomfortable with Shell 's strategy , he left the company in 2009 to start his consultancy Energy for One World , which is intended to help develop worldwide leadership to cope with " the global energy challenge " . <p> Asked about the Shell-view of the world , Kamp tells me the anecdote about the Oslo Energy Forum anecdote , and explains : " The oil companies say : the world needs energy , it needs oil and gas . That 's what we will deliver . It 's not up to us to change that . " <p> Do n't feel they threatened then , by investors who are taking money out of the fossil fuel sector , and by climate change measures ? " There are many inside Shell @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ eager to take on new challenges , who want to align the corporate agenda more with the national agenda . But oil and gas still are a money-making machine . Just like the banks . " Oil people still " live in a bubble " , says Kamp . " They lead a very good life . They operate in a very strange environment : the less they do , the more the price of their product goes up , and the more money they will make . That 's a very perverse incentive . " <p> Business cultures <p> Shell did spend considerable time and effort looking at alternative options in the past , Kamp acknowledges . " They were active in power production during the Enron time . They even partnered with Enron on some projects . They were active in advanced biofuels , solar power , offshore wind , nuclear , coal . The whole range of energy technologies has been inside the walls of Shell at one time or another . " <p> " They made the choice : let 's produce , market and trade LNG @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " <p> Yet these ventures never came to anything . There were two main reasons for this , says Kamp . " One : Shell is foremost a business , they operate on the shareholder model , so they go for the highest shareholder returns . These are in oil and gas , not in those other options . Two : Shell 's managers , although they are very capable , have a different DNA than which exists in these other business cultures . This is something you have to learn . It was a failure of leadership that they did not manage to do so . " <p> Kamp also saw that the Shell executives were in the end unable to look beyond their existing capabilities , based on oil and gas . " They were too enthusiastic about shale and not enthusiastic enough about electric cars . " <p> Thomas Hawk <p> In the end , says Kamp , Shell 's strategy boiled down to almost a single priority : LNG . " They made the choice : let 's produce , market and trade LNG . " <p> I recall reading articles by the fossil fuel guys that say they will make a quick switch to renewable energy when all the money dries up from the fossil fuels . They seem to think @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . The new companies are not bound by process and multi-layered management and can run circles around the incumbent companies who are slow to react . The experience is more akin to a high-end fashion boutique , albeit without the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ been uber-conscious of the brand experience they deliver from product to packaging , ecosystems , promotion , price and their retail environments whether it 's their own or via third parties . @@3217752 <h> Rundle : Nazi trial a triumph of our imagined good over evil <p> Oskar Groening has been sentenced to four years in prison , a sentence the 94-year-old may not live to see the end of . In this piece published earlier this year , Guy Rundle explains why good and evil are rarely black and white , and how an Auschwitz guard did what he could -- and much more than we would -- to stand up to the death camps . <p> Oskar Groening is perhaps not the Auschwitz guard we are looking for . The 94-year-old has been hiding in plain sight in Germany for decades . He has been threatened with prosecution before , but it has never gone ahead -- it has been said that the " political will was lacking " . <p> Groening was an attendant at Auschwitz charged with sorting and removing the luggage deposited by those who had arrived and were " selected " for immediate mass killing through gassing . Later he was responsible for taking the valuables found in such luggage and couriering them to Berlin @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to have him dubbed " the accountant of Auschwitz " , but this is aggrandising -- a sensationalist way of putting one of the last survivors of the era in the company of Mengele or Eichmann . There is no suggestion that Groening was directly involved in killing people , hustling them into the chambers , etc , or of sadistic behaviour . <p> Instead he is being charged with being a co-conspirator in the deaths of 300,000 people . His trial is to involve direct testimony by Auschwitz survivors . There is no cringing attempt to muddy the waters here : Greoning acknowledges he was there , and did what they said he did . He points out that he requested a transfer three times and was finally granted one , at which point he was sent to the Ardennes on the Western Front . <p> And thus , he is a big problem for all of us . <p> From the Nuremburg trials onwards , where a defence of " obeying orders " was overturned , there has been a disjuncture at the heart of genocide justice , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on key policymakers and implementers of the Holocaust , men either in , or one step down from , the core Nazi leadership . <p> Their " orders " defence was mostly bogus -- they were committed mass murderers . But the defence itself was ruled out in any case -- there was a moral duty to refuse to participate in the mass killing of people , even if your leader ordered you to do it . <p> The Nuremburg trials finished , and so did the process of de-Nazification in Occupied , and then , West Germany . People who had played leading roles in the Nazi apparatus slid back into business and politics . But after the trial of Eichmann , prosecutions re-commenced and began to extend to a wider group of people -- ordinary guards and subalterns who had been directly involved in the killings , commanding death squads , pushing people into chambers , etc . <p> This widening of the prosecution coincided with a renewed attention to the Holocaust in the 1970s . As memory of the total devastation and violence visited upon hundreds of millions @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ view of it -- Hogan 's Heroes , Dad 's Army , Monty Python , etc -- the radical evil of the Holocaust began to stand out more clearly . It is striking how little mention there is of it in contemporaneous accounts of 1944-45 , even by witnesses we celebrate as truth-tellers , such as George Orwell . <p> Yet by the late 1970s , with books , movies and TV series -- the 1979 US series Holocaust being a landmark , denounced as exploitation at the time , but the first way in which many of us encountered it -- the relationship between the war and the Holocaust reversed . The latter was the event , the war the mere pretext . The prosecutions of the foot soldiers in it -- many of them brutal racist and sadistic people -- increased . <p> But by moving to this lower level , prosecutions came into conflict with the " duress " principle of criminal law , that you were not responsible for your actions -- up to and including murder -- if you had a gun to your head . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ buried within it was clear : you had a right to try to live , to prefer your own life . Though it might well be moral to refuse , you were not legally required to do so . <h> 17 Responses <p> Good article . It reminds me that " angst " is a German word . @@3217788 <p> The recent slowdown in the rise of global average air temperatures during the first decade of the 21st century is being used as a touchstone argument for those who deny the science of global warming . But in research published today in Nature Climate Change , I and others show that the " hiatus " is just a blip on the radar compared to the long-term warming we have in store . <p> The slowdown or " hiatus " in warming refers to the period since 2001 , when despite ongoing increases in atmospheric greenhouse gases , Earth 's global average surface air temperature has remained more or less steady , warming by only around 0.1C . This contrasts with the 1990s , when warming reached more than 0.2C . <p> But by looking at around 200 projections from climate models , and separating those that capture the slowdown from those that do not , we have shown that the slowdown does not affect long-term warming projections in any measurable way . <p> More extreme climate change contrarians have seized upon this so-called " pause " as @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that we are headed into a period of global cooling . Considering that global average temperatures are still rising by a variety of measures , just more slowly , and as the last time we had a coldest year on record was 1909 , this is quite a leap of faith . <p> Unfortunately using global average surface air temperatures as a measure of total warming ignores the fact that most of the heat ( more than 93% ) goes into our oceans , which continue to warm without any sign of a pause , as you can see below . <h> Brakes on global warming ? <p> Global average surface air temperatures only reflect the heat present in the atmospheric layer immediately above the land/ocean surface . As heat gets sloshed around the oceans by processes such as El Ni ? o , the overlying atmosphere responds by tracking these heat variations . <p> As a result , surface temperature is strongly affected by natural variability . Beyond year-to-year variability such as El Ni ? o there are decade-to-decade changes , such as the Interdecadal Pacific Oscillation , which has @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ rise . <p> In particular the negative phase of the Interdecadal Pacific Oscillation can lead to dramatically increased trade winds and fewer El Ni ? os -- as has been occurring since 2001 . The modulation of these processes can significantly impact global average temperatures . <p> It is no coincidence that the only year outside the 21st century that remains in the top 10 hottest years on record is 1998 . That year saw the strongest El Ni ? o ever recorded -- a natural variation that added considerable heat to the 1998 result . <p> Ever since then climate change deniers have loved to produce graphs that start in 1998 , although when 2005 broke through as the new warmest year on record , another new favourite start year was born . Cherry-picking data at its worst . <p> And just for the record : 2010 then effectively tied with 2005 for the hottest year , and now 2014 has beaten the lot . And all the while the coldest year , 1909 , languishes as a fading memory of a time long past , predating human 's undeniable @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ certain to stand for a long time to come . <p> While 1998 was a warm year for the surface atmosphere , in the oceans , where most of the warming is going , the story is different . Looking at ocean temperatures down to 2 km , 1998 does not even make the cut for the top 10 . Instead , total annual average ocean heat content has increased steadily during the hiatus , at quite a confronting rate given that this metric is closely tied to global sea-level rise . <p> The strong influence of natural variability on surface air temperatures is the reason that climate researchers regularly point out that any record shorter than around 20-30 years is not useful for detecting long-term trends associated with anthropogenic warming . <p> Step back from these short time scales and look at the past century to see the clear human influence . <p> A man and his dog explain climate statistics <h> Sifting the signal from the noise <p> Climate modellers have run hundreds of future warming simulations . Some of these simulations capture the slowdown if they happen to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> But if you average all the model projections , the warming predicted for the past decade outpaces observations . This has been argued as evidence that scientists have overstated the threats posed by increasing concentrations of greenhouse gases . <p> Yet the climate science community has repeatedly shown compelling evidence that this slowdown reflects decade-to-decade variability . <p> Until now , however , no evaluation has been made of the possible consequences for long-term projections . Specifically , if the variability controlling the current hiatus is linked to longer-term sequestration of heat into the deep ocean , this might require us to recalibrate future projections . <p> With this in mind , we decided to test whether 21st century warming projections are altered in any way when considering only simulations that capture a slowdown in global surface warming , as observed since 2001 . <p> As you can see in our paper we looked at this by separating all available future projections into two groups - those that captured the current slowdown and those that did not . We then compared the warming throughout the 21st Century for both @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ from the latest IPCC report . The first scenario assumes greenhouse gas concentrations continue to rise unabated through the 21st century . The second assumes emissions are reduced to address global warming , peaking by 2040 before declining sharply . <p> It turns out that the difference between the " slowdown " set and the rest of the projections was negligible by the mid-21st century . And the longer the projections go on , the smaller the difference gets . <p> Under the high emissions scenario , for example , the difference in average projected end-of-century warming between the two groups of models is less than 0.1C ; a tiny fraction of the projected 5C global warming if emissions are not curbed . <p> This clearly shows that the impact of the current hiatus is effectively non-existent in the context of long term warming . <h> Do n't wait for the warming to return <p> It is not news to climate scientists that natural variability impacts the short-term movement of global average temperatures in both directions . We have seen short-term pauses and even short-term reversals in global temperature rise in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ long term warming trend . <p> We have also seen periods of more rapid surface atmosphere warming . In effect the surface warming comes in spurts , facilitated by phase shifts in the Interdecadal Pacific Oscillation . <p> And over time , despite the cycles of warming and cooling due to this oscillation , despite solar minima , despite cooling from volcanic eruptions and cooling from the massive loading in the atmosphere of anthropogenic aerosols , the trend in temperatures above the noise clearly continues upwards . <p> Our research shows that limiting the projections to models that capture the current slowdown in no way alters long-term projections . We can thus place extra confidence in the synthesised projections of the IPCC . <p> Limiting warming to 2C is a laudable aspiration -- one that gives us a decent chance ( not certainty ) of avoiding dangerous interference with the climate system . Unfortunately if we hang around talking about a 15-year slowdown for too long , the chance to limit warming to less than the 2C threshold will rapidly disappear .

@@3217892 <h> Michael Dobie <p> His name is Sudan . He 's 42 years old and lives in Kenya , protected 24/7 by armed guards . <p> Sudan is a northern white rhinoceros , the last known male of his kind in the world . Think about that . The last . <p> What the pace of extinction says about us is disturbing . So many of our species do n't seem alarmed about our scary ability to eliminate other species . <p> He lives with two females at a conservatory . A tiny handful of female northern white rhinos - perhaps five - live elsewhere in captivity . That 's it . <p> Which means the survival of this subspecies is up to Sudan . Who is old . With a low sperm count . And , perhaps , too weak to mount a female . <p> Advertisement <p> Sudan is the forlorn face of a crisis largely of our own making - the crisis of extinction . <p> Earth is experiencing a mass extinction of animals and plants , the worst since the dinosaurs vanished 65 million @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ True , extinction is a natural phenomenon - one to five species disappear naturally per year . But scientists estimate we 're now losing dozens of species every day , many we know nothing about . <p> The reasons are familiar , often interrelated and all about us humans . We cut down forests and plow prairies . We overharvest fish and mine the ocean floor . We pollute and produce devastating climate change . And we hunt . <p> Poaching decimated many species of rhinos , and elephants , too . The northern white rhino 's horn is prized in Asia for its supposed medicinal properties , despite research debunking virtually all of those claims . Sudan 's horn has been removed for his protection . <p> What the pace of extinction says about us is disturbing . So many of our species do n't seem alarmed about our scary ability to eliminate other species . We muster protest for nature 's icons , like elephants or apes . But we shrug when an insect or plant vanishes . We 're humans , it 's our world , get out @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Sometimes , the effects of our actions are plain to see . Take the wolves of Yellowstone National Park , hunted and poisoned to extermination in the park by 1926 . Without wolves , Yellowstone 's elk population boomed , with disastrous effects . Elk are voracious grazers . The decline of vegetation led to the disappearance of songbirds and beavers . Without the ponds created by beaver dams , otters , muskrats and moose declined . So did mice , voles and pronghorn fawns , prey for the coyote that blossomed without the wolves . When the wolves were reintroduced in 1995 , the damage was reversed . <p> Nature establishes balances we should respect . Extermination throws that out of whack . Already , scientists worry about the variety of life in Africa 's savanna grasslands , maintained in part by rhino feasting . <p> Just as troubling is when we do n't know what we 're losing . Literally , do ... not ... know . <p> Plants , for example , are a huge source of medicinal drugs for everything from malaria to pain . Rainforest plants @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ become dietary supplements . <p> The non-reflective eyes of moths are inspiring designs for more efficient solar panels . Dirt from a grassy field in Maine harbored an antibiotic that killed the staph infection MRSA and drug-resistant tuberculosis in tests on mice . <p> Why do we snicker when environmentalists worry about developments that threaten endangered species like the tiger salamander or snail darter ? Our ecosystems are complex . One extinction can lead to another . How much do you really want to kiss goodbye ? <p> It 's an amazing world out there . We do n't even know how amazing it is yet .

@@3222790 <h> Plants : From Roots to Riches . Part 5 of 5 <p> Kathy Willis examines how the technology that helped map whole genomes in plants and animals was to revolutionise the classification of flowering plants ; the evolution of our rainforests as revealed by DNA fingerprinting ; plants as essential regulators of our planet 's atmospheric carbon and water cycles ; how green spaces and ecosystems have a positive effect on our health and well being ; and the future role of plants as providers of food to feed the planet 's growing population . <p> Kathy Willis is Professor of Long-Term Ecology at Oxford University and a fellow of Merton College , Oxford . Winner of several awards , she has spent over 20 years researching and teaching biodiversity and conservation at Oxford and Cambridge . <h> Transcript <p> David Fisher : This is The Science Show , hello , I 'm David Fisher . The first four episodes of Roots to Riches were broadcast in January and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ this series on the history of botany shows how investments in plants , roots , yields untold wealth , riches . Richard Barley , director of horticulture , is here with me . <p> Richard Barley : Well , this area was laid @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ with a series of beds that he called the herbaceous grounds , to show the classification and relationships of different plants . And then by the time his son became director , Joseph Hooker , and he teamed up with George Bentham , they came up with a modified version , if you like , of that plant classification system , the Bentham-Hooker version . And so the beds were re-ordered according to that notion of understanding . <p> Kathy Willis : The process they used to work out how plants were related had all the beauty of simplicity . They looked at them , compared visible details like shape , colour , size layout and the number of various plant parts , and you could work out who was related to whom . It was a brilliant system . But inevitably it had its limitations . However , it provided an important framework that taxonomists continued to build on , and argue over , for the next century . <p> But it was n't until the 1990s with the opportunities offered by the new science of DNA sequencing that the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ developed . For the first time , this mapping allowed classification to be made at a molecular level , rather than just based on appearance . Scientists began by sequencing a single gene common to all flowering plants to see how much change in the DNA there was between different species . This work was led here at Kew by Professor Mark Chase , keeper of the Jodrell Laboratory . <p> Mark Chase : It was a big experiment , it was 500 sequences of a single gene that covered most angiosperms , the gene that takes CO2 out of the atmosphere and pulls it into the photosynthetic pathway that leads to the production of sugars . So it 's an absolutely essential gene for anything that undergoes photosynthesis . And once the field started to move into this high gear , so to speak , it really went quickly , and the technology that was developed for the human genome project , the sequencers , are then available to us and we can produce all these data . And most of us were surprised when we found out that the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ actually interdigitated with things that were thought to be in other families . <p> Kathy Willis : But the new techniques also allowed scientists to begin to see new connections between plants , which appearance alone could never have suggested . So at the family level there was n't a great deal of discordance . Above the level of family , things were wildly different . <p> So , for example , the really classic one that everybody cites as being almost incomprehensible is the relationship between the protea family , Proteaceae , the plane @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Nelumbonaceae . And I brought this just to illustrate the fact that when we did all this DNA work on palms , here you have a great example of the fan palms , this Kerriodoxa here has this epically beautiful , perfect , rounded fan-shaped leaf , and yet above it this slightly scruffy fishtail palm , it 's hard to believe that that fishtail palm is pretty closely related to this fan palm here . And some of those findings were really unexpected and turned our concepts of palm classification on their head . <p> Kathy Willis : So if you classified them according to leaf shape , then you 'd put them into one group , but then when you look at their molecular characteristics they are not so close , is that correct ? <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is positively misleading and does n't necessarily reflect the history underpinning that group . <p> Kathy Willis : In looking at the history of palms , DNA sequences are themselves an amazing evolutionary record . They show mutations that have accumulated in genomes over millions of years , in a sense a molecular fossil record . <p> One of the principal advantages of genetic analysis is the length of the timescales involved . Looking at living specimens or examples from field collections gives you only very recent history , a snapshot in time . Even fossils only get you so far , and there is also bias in the record depending on the preservation conditions at the time of burial and the potential of the plant to become fossilised . Some plants are much more suitable for fossilisation than others . Genetic analysis therefore provides an additional window into evolution over much longer timescales and crucially begins to show when diversification of palms occurred . <p> William Baker : We can do clever things with DNA trees nowadays , a bit of smoke and mirrors , and using complex analysis and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ estimate a date for the diversification of the palms . And we think the living lineages of palms that we see today started to diversify about 100 million years ago , so that 's in the middle of the Cretaceous , and because they have this very strong relationship with rainforest . 90% , 95% of all palms occur in rainforests . That has led us on to wonder , well , if we have a really good evolutionary tree for the palms , what can we get out of that in terms of insights into the evolution of rainforest itself ? <p> One of the things that first hit us is that if palms have always been conserved to occur in the rainforest , they diversified 100 million years ago and yet the really robust fossil evidence for angiosperm dominated rainforest , such as we know today , does n't really kick in until after the end of the Cretaceous , 55 million , 65 million years ago . And yet our evidence seems to suggest that palms were diversifying in a rainforest-like environment 100 million years ago . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the molecular records suggest as the earliest diversification of palms and what the fossil record indicates is just one of a number of conundrums that have been thrown up by analysing the DNA fingerprint . Another key question that botanists have long wondered is how rainforest became so rich in species . They are , after all , the most plant species rich areas on the planet . <p> Charles Darwin 's contemporary , Alfred Russel Wallace , suggested in his Tropical Nature , and Other Essays of 1878 that the great mass of equatorial forests must have benefited from consistent climatic conditions over time , whereas Earth 's temperate areas had experienced periodic checks and extinctions . <p> Reading : In one , evolution has had a fair chance ; in the other , it has had countless difficulties thrown in its way . The equatorial regions are then , as regards their past and present life history , a more ancient world than that represented by the temperate zones , a world in which the laws that have governed the progressive development of life have operated with comparatively little @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ infinitely varied and beautiful forms . <p> Kathy Willis : This is now known as the museum model of evolution , where it 's argued that constant climatic conditions and stability over time allows genetic variations to accumulate , leading to greater species richness in these regions . So what does the genetic evidence suggest ? <p> William Baker : What we found in the palms is that the diversity of lineages accumulated in a very steady rate over time , at least up until the Miocene about 20 million years ago . And that 's exciting because most of the molecular evidence is pointing us to sort of glitzy , recent , rapid radiations . And also we know that rainforests are dynamic places and there 's a lot of disturbance and turnover over time , and yet our evidence matches the so-called museum model . Diversity can evolve and build over time . So to find out that palms conform to that was extremely exciting . But we have n't yet looked at the tips of the tree of life , we have n't got into the species-level detail @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ we are the moment for example at the radiation of palms in Madagascar , we will start to see some of those explosive , rapid periods of diversification happening in more recent timeframes . <p> Kathy Willis : Food , timber , medicines and writing materials have all been derived from palms . And the more we know about them , the more useful they become , and the more we understand the need to protect and nurture them , for their benefit and ours . <p> So next we turn to the services plants offer , in effect the natural capital of plants . Which plant biodiversity is most useful to humankind , both now and in the future ? And which bits should we conserve , and where ? <p> 2010 was declared the International Year of Biodiversity . In this year , a study was undertaken in which UK citizens were asked to define the word ' biodiversity ' . The results were rather surprising , not least that the most common answer was that biodiversity was some kind of washing powder . In fact biodiversity is the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ from genes through to populations , communities and whole ecosystems . But given the global challenges that we face in terms of climate change , a growing population , urbanisation , food and fuel security , it is perhaps understandable that many of us do n't appreciate the term ' biodiversity ' , and even if we do , we then question whether biodiversity is a luxury we can no longer afford . <p> It 's the ' so what ' question ; why should we conserve biodiversity ? Perhaps the answer lies in properly assessing how biodiversity serves our livelihoods and well-being . It 's an approach that is starting to move up the political agenda globally . Take , for example , the United Nations global outlook report in 2008 . <p> Reading : Biodiversity plays fundamental roles in maintaining and enhancing the well-being of the world 's more than 6.7 billion people , rich and poor , rural and urban alike . <p> Kathy Willis : So in effect it 's an approach that suggests we value biodiversity according to its contribution to humankind , so-called ecosystem services @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ model for biodiversity conservation , where landscapes with the highest concentration of biodiversity are conserved . <p> Just imagine a photo of a landscape with a patch of forest to one side , fields in the middle , trees around the field margins and mountains in the background . In a traditional protected area categorisation you would probably place your protected area fence around the forest on the basis that this almost certainly is the most species rich part of the landscape . But if we are assessing the biodiversity of this landscape in terms of the services that it provides the livelihoods and well-being , the forest now represents an important regulating service for drawing down carbon dioxide , the trees around the edge of the field are important for nesting habitats for pollinators , the fields themselves are important for providing crops , and the mountains for recreation and mental well-being . So the landscape becomes partitioned in a very different way . <p> It 's a new approach to biodiversity conservation that was first highlighted in a landmark study called the Millennium Ecosystem Assessment in 2005 in which @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ lines of inquiry . First , what aspects of biodiversity are the most important to conserve for the ecosystem services they provide ? And second , where are these located ? <p> One of the biggest environmental risks facing humankind today is climate change . Much of this is being driven by increasing atmospheric carbon dioxide which is now at 400 parts per million volume . This is approximately a third higher than any other time in the past 800,000 years . Leaving aside the cause of this increase , we urgently need to find mechanisms to reduce the amount of carbon dioxide in the atmosphere , and this is where plants come in . Trees are some of the most effective organisms at drawing down atmospheric carbon dioxide . Their essential role in regulating the atmosphere has been known for a long time . But which trees are best at doing this , and where ? Two questions I have put to Yadvinder Malhi , Professor of Terrestrial Ecosystems in the Environmental Change Institute at the University of Oxford . Yadvinder runs a large global tree monitoring network for tropical @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Yadvinder Malhi : The tropical forests play a huge role because they are amongst the most productive ecosystems on Earth , so about 40% to 50% of the carbon that flows through the rainforest biosphere . And also because they sit on the equator that 's where all the energy is , so that 's where the forests affect the amount of water cycling back to the atmosphere and the energy balance of the Earth and the global climate . So they really are engines of global climate . <p> Kathy Willis : Which are the most important trees , would you say ... I presume they are tropical trees that draw down CO2 ? <p> Yadvinder Malhi : The ideal tree for drawing down CO2 is something that grows fast but then lives for a long time and locks in that carbon and holds it for centuries or millennia . And the tropics centre have that combination , but other trees such as the redwood in California are also important in that respect , but in the tropics one example would be something like a Brazil nut tree which @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ live for centuries . Globally the tropical forests pulls down the most atmospheric carbon dioxide , followed by temperate forests and then arboreal forests . Interestingly however , tropical forests that are recovering from past deforestation and logging provide the largest sink , accounting for up to 1.7 billion tonnes of carbon drawdown a year . <p> Yadvinder Malhi : They are the ones that can really accumulate carbon if they are left to persist for a long time . In the tropics we tend to view landscapes that are deforested as somehow lost , somehow they 've lost their virginity or their pristineness , and actually that 's a wrong view . These are landscapes of incredible potential and resilience . <p> Yadvinder Malhi : It shows the importance of also putting attention to second growth @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ detract from the importance of the primary forests , they have huge value as carbon stores . But new research is also suggesting that in addition to the regulation of global carbon budgets , trees could also be extremely important in regulating global water circulation . <p> Yadvinder Malhi : If you have an area , say a deforested area , a cattle branch in the Amazon without trees , when rain comes down it enters the soil and then it ends up in the groundwater and out in the rivers and disappears to the ocean . And as the clouds form above the tropics , they release heat into the upper atmosphere , and that is one of the main engines of the global atmospheric circulation . So changes in forest cover and cloud cover in the Amazon can end up affecting rainfall in Europe and Central Asia through these large-scale connections . <p> Kathy Willis : So we can regulate atmospheric carbon dioxide by understanding which trees absorb it most efficiently and where , and we can even influence rainfall patterns in Europe and Asia by how we treat tropical rainforest . These examples represent just the tiny fraction of the natural capital of plants in regulating Earth 's systems . These can be seen on satellite imagery all over the world . They often represent small islands within vast areas of urban or agricultural sprawl . The reason for their existence is often a legacy of the past . Often , but not always , these areas are relatively poor in biodiversity . But whereas the earliest expeditions of Banks and Hooker were mainly focused on finding the best plants , we have to thank another botanist for promoting the need to conserve a different aspect of biodiversity to mitigate against plant disease and climate change . It was the Russian botanist Nikolai Vavilov who recognised the importance of finding the closest wild relatives to many of our staple crops , be it cereals , legumes , vegetables or fibre-producing plants . His vision was profound and required not just an understanding of the location of the best crops but also identification and distribution of their closest wild relatives . <p> Vavilov would I hope be relieved to see that his vision flourishes in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ shock him however is how valuable in monetary terms the natural capital of crop wild relatives are now . In a landmark study , Richard Thompson from the global accounting firm PricewaterhouseCoopers , made a preliminary calculation of the monetary value of genetically diverse plants which are the wild relatives of our modern crops . <p> Richard Thompson : We gathered as much data as we could to demonstrate what was the yield improvement , and then we translated that in effect into a big financial model which says the impact on yield is x , and then we were building up a set of assumptions in order to try and identify exactly what proportion of the yield improvement is observed within the crop . And then we converted that into dollars in terms of benefit to production at the factory gate . <p> Kathy Willis : So which crops were you looking at ? <p> Richard Thompson : Well , we looked at three crops to begin with ; wheat , rice and potatoes . We picked those because those are crops that are grown globally , and we also @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ already . The work on yams also illustrates one final aspect of biodiversity that we must conserve if we are to build resilience into our ecosystems , and that is genetic diversity . <p> Genetic diversity at the level of the individual species , within populations across species ranges and resulting from millions of years of evolutionary processes is our ultimate get-out-of-jail card when faced with the need for resilient ecosystems .
